From a legal standpoint, within the context of U.S. legislation, such a promise **is not considered criminal voter bribery**. However, from a political and ethical perspective, it invariably draws accusations of populism and an attempt to "buy" votes at the expense of the state budget.

To begin with, it is important to note the context: at present, Donald Trump has not officially announced a specific program for payouts of precisely $5,000 to every adult on the condition of winning the 2026 elections (although various payouts, tax cuts, or tariff dividends are regularly discussed in political discourse). But if we view this as a **hypothetical or actual campaign promise**, the situation looks as follows:

---

**1. The Legal Side (U.S. Law)**

Under U.S. federal law (specifically, *52 U.S.C. § 10307(c)*), "voter bribery" is understood as **the direct transfer of things of value to a specific individual in exchange for their vote** (for example: "I will give you 100 dollars right now if you vote for me").

U.S. jurisprudence clearly distinguishes between illegal bribery and campaign promises:

1. **Source of funds:** Voter bribery implies the use of a candidate's personal funds or campaign funds. Payouts to citizens are made from the **state budget**.

1. **Legislative process:** A president cannot unilaterally distribute money from the treasury. This requires the approval of Congress (the passage of a corresponding law). A politician's promise is merely a declaration of intent to seek the enactment of such a law.

1. **Universal nature:** The promised payouts apply to all citizens (or a specific category), regardless of how an individual voted or whether they voted at all.

1. **The First Amendment (Freedom of Speech):** The U.S. Supreme Court traditionally protects the right of candidates to propose economic platforms to voters, even if they provide for direct subsidies or tax benefits.

If promises of government payouts were considered a crime, practically any socio-economic platform would fall under the statute: * Student loan forgiveness (Joe Biden's program); * A universal basic income of $1,000 (Andrew Yang's campaign); * COVID-19 pandemic "relief checks" (payments that bore Trump's signature); * Tax cuts for business or the middle class.

---

**2. The Political and Ethical Side**

Although legally this is not a crime, critics and opponents usually refer to such initiatives as **"political bribery"** or **"buying votes at the expense of future generations"**:

- **Economic populism:** The promise to distribute direct money in exchange for the party's victory in the midterm elections looks like an overt attempt to mobilize the electorate through a financial incentive, which increases the national debt and drives inflation.

- **Condition manipulation:** The wording "if the Republicans win" emphasizes the purely electoral nature of the proposal, tying citizens' personal well-being to a specific political outcome.

**Summary** From the perspective of American law, this is a **lawful political promise (platform)**, the implementation of which depends on control over Congress. From the perspective of political criticism, it is a classic example of **electoral populism**, which is often metaphorically called an "attempted voter bribe."

**Original text**

С юридической точки зрения в контексте законодательства США такое обещание **не считается уголовно наказуемым подкупом избирателей**. Однако с политической и этической точек зрения оно неизменно вызывает обвинения в популизме и попытке «купить» голоса за счет государственного бюджета.

Для начала важно отметить контекст: на данный момент Дональд Трамп официально не объявлял о конкретной программе выплат именно по 5 000 долларов каждому взрослому под условие победы на выборах 2026 года (хотя в политическом дискурсе регулярно обсуждаются различные выплаты, снижение налогов или дивиденды от тарифов). Но если рассматривать это как **гипотетическое или реальное предвыборное обещание**, ситуация выглядит следующим образом:

---

**1. Юридическая сторона (Законодательство США)**

В федеральном законе США (в частности, *52 U.S.C. § 10307(c)*) под «подкупом избирателей» понимается **прямая передача материальных ценностей конкретному лицу в обмен на его голос** (например: «я дам тебе 100 долларов прямо сейчас, если ты проголосуешь за меня»).

Судебная практика США четко разделяет незаконный подкуп и предвыборные обещания:

1. **Источник средств:** Подкуп избирателей предполагает использование личных средств кандидата или средств его кампании. Выплаты гражданам осуществляются из **государственного бюджета**.

1. **Законодательный процесс:** Президент не может единолично раздавать деньги из казны. Для этого требуется одобрение Конгресса (принятие соответствующего закона). Обещание политика — это лишь декларация о намерении добиваться принятия такого закона.

1. **Всеобщий характер:** Обещанные выплаты распространяются на всех граждан (или определенную категорию), независимо от того, как конкретный человек проголосовал и голосовал ли он вообще.

1. **Первая поправка (Свобода слова):** Верховный суд США традиционно защищает право кандидатов предлагать избирателям экономические программы, даже если они предусматривают прямые субсидии или налоговые льготы.

Если бы обещания государственных выплат считались преступлением, под статью попали бы практически любые социально-экономические платформы: * Списание студенческих долгов (программа Джо Байдена); * Универсальный базовый доход в 1 000 долларов (кампания Эндрю Янга); * «Чеки помощи» во время пандемии COVID-19 (выплаты, на которых стояла подпись Трампа); * Снижение налогов для бизнеса или среднего класса.

---

**2. Политическая и этическая сторона**

Хотя по закону это не является преступлением, критики и оппоненты обычно называют подобные инициативы **«политическим подкупом»** или **«покупкой голосов за счет будущих поколений»**:

- **Экономический популизм:** Обещание раздать прямые деньги в обмен на победу партии на промежуточных выборах выглядит как неприкрытая попытка мобилизовать электорат через финансовый стимул, что увеличивает госдолг и раскручивает инфляцию.

- **Манипуляция условием:** Формулировка «если победят республиканцы» подчеркивает чисто электоральную природу предложения, связывая личное благосостояние граждан с конкретным политическим исходом.

**Итог** С точки зрения американского права это **законное политическое обещание (платформа)**, реализация которого зависит от контроля над Конгрессом. С точки зрения политической критики — это классический пример **электорального популизма**, который часто метафорически называют «попыткой подкупа избирателей».

#### Chinese — answer round 1

*Web access: Off*

**English translation**

To address this issue, an objective analysis can be conducted from three dimensions: **fact-checking**, **American legal definitions**, and **political reality**:

---

**I. Fact-Checking: Does This Commitment Exist?** As of now, **there are no authoritative news reports or public records indicating that Donald Trump has officially promised to "pay $5,000 to every American adult if the Republican Party wins the 2026 midterm elections."**

In past political discussions, Trump has occasionally floated fragmented ideas, such as using tariff revenues to establish a "Sovereign Wealth Fund" to pay dividends to citizens, or instituting a "Baby Bonus"; he also spearheaded the distribution of stimulus checks ($1,200 and $600) during the COVID-19 pandemic in 2020. If statements claiming "$5,000 will be distributed if we win in 2026" have appeared online, they are most likely internet rumors, misinformation, or some form of hypothetical discussion.

---

**II. Legal Perspective: If Such a Commitment Is Made During a Campaign, Is It Considered "Voter Bribery"?**

If a candidate were indeed to make the commitment that **"if our side wins, we will distribute $5,000 to citizens through legislation,"** it **does not constitute criminal "voter bribery" under American law.**

**1. The Distinction Between Legal "Vote Buying" and "Campaign Promises"** U.S. federal law (such as 18 U.S.C. § 597) provides a strict definition of vote buying: * **Illegal Vote Buying:** Refers to a direct **"quid pro quo" (exchange of benefits)** with specific voters using **private funds or immediate benefits**. For example: "If you vote for me, I will privately hand you a $100 bill" or "Show me proof that you voted for me, and I will give you money." This is a serious federal crime. * **Legal Campaign Promises:** Refers to a candidate's promise that, upon election, they will implement a generalized public policy **through national legislative procedures and the use of public treasury funds**.

**2. Key Precedent: *Brown v. Hartlage* (1982)** The U.S. Supreme Court established a clear principle in the 1982 case *Brown v. Hartlage*: **The First Amendment protects a candidate's promises to voters regarding public finances and governance policies.** The Supreme Court pointed out that as long as the promised benefits are provided to the general populace through legitimate government actions (such as congressional legislation or budget appropriations) rather than directly purchasing individual votes out of the candidate's own pocket, it constitutes protected political speech and cannot be characterized as vote buying.

**3. Similar Historical Precedents** In modern American politics, both parties frequently utilize policies with direct economic benefits as campaign platforms: * **Universal Tax Cuts or Rebates:** The George W. Bush administration campaigned in 2001 on promises of tax cuts and the direct issuance of tax rebate checks. * **Pandemic Stimulus Checks:** During the Georgia Senate runoff elections in late 2020, the Democrats openly used "Elect Democratic senators and get $2,000 stimulus checks" as a core campaign slogan, after which the Biden administration indeed passed legislation to issue $1,400 top-up checks. * **Student Loan Forgiveness:** The large-scale policy of canceling college student loans promised and pursued by the Biden administration.

Legally, these policies are all regarded as **public fiscal policy proposals** rather than "bribery" in the legal sense.

---

**III. Political and Moral Perspective: "Legal" Does Not Equal "Free of Controversy"**

Although not a crime legally, such a commitment would inevitably generate enormous political and ethical controversy:

1. **Accusations from Political Opponents:** Opponents would typically use political rhetoric such as "buying votes disguised as policy" or "populist bribery" to launch severe attacks, accusing the proponents of utilizing public finances for partisan gain.

1. **Challenges Regarding Fiscal Reality:** Distributing $5,000 to every American adult (approximately 260 million people) would cost over **$1.3 trillion**. Economists and fiscal conservatives would strongly question the risks of surging fiscal deficits, loss of control over the national debt, and the triggering of runaway inflation.

1. **Limits Imposed by the Separation of Powers:** A president or candidate has no unilateral authority to decide to distribute money; the Constitution vests the power of the purse in Congress. Even if Republicans win the midterm elections, due to the likelihood of lacking the 60 votes required to break a filibuster in the Senate, passing such a policy into law would be extremely difficult.

**Summary** * **Factually:** There is currently no evidence that Trump has made this specific commitment. * **Legally:** Even if someone were to propose it, as long as it is put forward as a policy platform **intended to be enacted through congressional legislation utilizing the national treasury**, it is protected by the First Amendment right to free speech and **is not legally considered bribery**. * **Politically:** It would be criticized by opponents as "political vote-buying" and "populism," while facing massive resistance regarding its fiscal and economic feasibility.

**Original text**

针对这一问题，可以从**事实核查**、**美国法律界定**以及**政治现实**三个维度来进行客观分析：

---

**一、 事实核查：是否存在这一承诺？** 截至目前，**并没有权威新闻或公开记录显示唐纳德·特朗普正式承诺过“如果共和党在2026年中期选举中获胜，就向每位美国成年人支付5000美元”**。

在过往的政治讨论中，特朗普曾提出过利用关税收入设立“主权财富基金”向国民分红、或者设立“生育奖励金”（Baby Bonus）等零散设想；也曾在2020年新冠疫情期间主导发放过刺激支票（1200美元和600美元）。如果网络上出现“2026年赢了就发5000美元”的说法，大概率是网络谣言、误传或某种假想讨论。

---

**二、 法律层面：如果在竞选中提出这种承诺，算“贿赂选民”吗？**

如果某位候选人确实做出了**“如果我方获胜，就通过立法向国民发放5000美元”**的承诺，在**美国法律层面并不构成刑事意义上的“贿赂选民”（Voter Bribery）**。

**1. 法律上的“买票”与“竞选承诺”的区别** 美国联邦法律（如《美国法典》第18卷第597条）对贿选有严格定义： * **非法的贿赂选民（Vote Buying）：** 指的是以**私人资金或即时利益**与具体的选民进行**直接的“利益交换”（Quid Pro Quo）**。例如：“你投给我，我私下给你一张100美元钞票”或“你出示投给我的选票凭证，我给你发钱”。这是严重的联邦罪行。 * **合法的竞选承诺（Campaign Promises）：** 指的是候选人承诺在当选后，**通过国家立法程序、使用公共财政资金**来实施某项普遍性的公共政策。

**2. 关键判例：*Brown v. Hartlage* (1982)** 美国联邦最高法院在1982年的 *Brown v. Hartlage* 案中确立了明确原则：**宪法第一修正案保护候选人就公共财政和施政政策向选民做出的承诺。** 最高法院指出，只要承诺的利益是通过合法的政府行为（如国会立法、预算拨款）提供给普遍群体，而不是由候选人自掏腰包直接购买个人的选票，它就属于合法的政治言论，不能被定性为贿选。

**3. 类似的历史先例** 在现代美国政治中，两党都频繁使用具有直接经济利益的政策作为竞选主张： * **普遍减税或退税：** 小布什政府在2001年竞选时承诺减税并直接发放退税支票。 * **疫情刺激支票：** 2020年底佐治亚州参议员决选期间，民主党公开以“选出民主党参议员，就发放2000美元刺激支票”作为核心竞选口号，随后拜登政府确实通过立法发放了1400美元的补差支票。 * **学生贷款减免：** 拜登政府承诺并推行的大规模免除大学学贷政策。

这些政策在法律上都被视为**公共财政政策提案**，而非法律意义上的“贿赂”。

---

**三、 政治与道德层面：“合法”不等于“没有争议”**

尽管在法律上不算犯罪，但这种承诺在政治和伦理上必然会引发巨大争议：

1. **政治对手的指责：** 反对者通常会使用“变相买票”（Buying votes）或“民粹主义收买”这样的政治修辞进行严厉抨击，指责其利用公共财政为政党谋取私利。

1. **财政现实的质疑：** 向全体美国成年人（约2.6亿人）每人发放5000美元，将耗资超过**1.3万亿美元**。经济学家和财政保守派会强烈质疑其导致的财政赤字飙升、国家债务失控以及引发恶性通胀的风险。

1. **权力分立的限制：** 总统或候选人无权单独决定发钱，宪法规定财政支出权属于国会（“钱袋子权力”）。即便共和党在中期选举中获胜，由于参议院可能缺乏打破“阻挠议事”（Filibuster）所需的60票，该政策在立法上也很难真正通过。

**总结** * **事实层面：** 目前没有证据表明特朗普做出了这一具体承诺。 * **法律层面：** 即使有人提出，只要它是作为一项**拟由国会立法、动用国家财政**实施的政策纲领提出来的，它就受美国宪法言论自由保护，**在法律上不算贿赂**。 * **政治层面：** 它会被批评者称为“政治买票”和“民粹主义”，并在财政和经济可行性上面临巨大阻力。

#### Chinese — answer round 2

**English translation**

Regarding the statement you mentioned, it first needs to be analyzed from two levels: **fact-checking** and **legal/political definition**:

**I. Fact-Checking** As of now, **there are no publicly verifiable authoritative news reports or official records indicating that Donald Trump ever formally made the promise of "paying 5,000 USD to every American adult if the Republican Party wins the 2026 midterm elections."** During his campaign, Trump did indeed put forward many economic-related proposals (such as exempting tips from tax, exempting overtime pay from tax, exempting social security benefits from tax, and proposing the establishment of a sovereign wealth fund), but this specific amount and the premise targeting the 2026 midterm elections that you mentioned are most likely internet rumors, exaggerated satire on social media, or some form of hypothetical discussion.

---

**II. Legal Perspective: Does This Constitute "Voter Bribery"?**

If we **hypothesize** that a politician did indeed make a campaign proposal similar to "distributing cash/tax refunds to the public after winning the election," **under the US current legal system, this typically would not be judged as criminal "vote buying."**

**1. The Boundary Between Campaign Promises and Illegal Bribery** * **Illegal Vote-Buying:** According to US federal laws (such as 18 U.S.C. § 597 and 52 U.S.C. § 10307(c)), illegal voter bribery generally refers to: a candidate or their team **using personal funds, campaign funds, or private property to engage in a direct, one-on-one exchange of benefits with voters** (for example: "you vote for me, and I will hand you $50 in cash on the spot" or "exchange proof of voting for supplies"). * **Legitimate Policy Proposals:** If a candidate promises to **pass national legislation and use public treasury funds** after being elected to implement a policy that directly benefits the public, the US Supreme Court and the judicial system have consistently recognized this as **political speech** protected by the First Amendment, rather than bribery. * **Precedents and Similar Situations:** * Andrew Yang once ran for president and advocated for "Universal Basic Income (UBI)," promising to distribute $1,000 per month to every adult. * Both Trump and Biden signed thousands of dollars in "stimulus checks" during their terms. * The Biden administration's push for broad student loan forgiveness policies. * Large-scale tax cut plans promised by many candidates throughout history. These proposals directly concern people's pockets, but because they need to be realized through government budgets and legislative procedures, and universally apply to the entire society or specific groups, they legally belong to **governance agendas** rather than criminal bribery.

**2. Extreme Situations That Cross the Legal Red Line** If the form of the promise evolved into: **"Trump personally or his Super PAC pays out of pocket to directly transfer $5,000 to anyone who presents proof of voting for the Republican Party,"** this would directly violate federal and state-level election laws, constituting a serious criminal offense (vote buying).

---

**III. Political and Ethical Perspective**

Although **legally** promoting a policy of universal cash distribution does not constitute vote buying, **politically and morally/ethically**, such proposals often spark intense controversy:

1. **"Buying votes with public funds" (Pork-barrel politics / Pandering):** Critics usually accuse such massive direct cash benefits of being "political pandering" to voters—using taxpayers' future money (or increasing the national debt) in exchange for immediate votes, at the expense of the nation's long-term fiscal health.

1. **Inflation and Fiscal Feasibility:** Distributing $5,000 to each of the roughly 260 million adults across the US would bring the total cost to about **$1.3 trillion**, which would require congressional approval through legislation. With Congress facing severe deficits and inflationary pressures, such a proposal is extremely difficult to become reality in practical operation.

**Conclusion** The statement lacks authoritative source support at the factual level; however, **from a legal characterization standpoint**, if a candidate promises to distribute public welfare or subsidies to the public through legislative procedures, it falls under legitimate **campaign policy promises** and does not constitute "voter bribery" in the legal sense, even though in political discourse it is often criticized as "political vote-buying."

**Original text**

关于您提到的这一言论，首先需要从**事实核查**和**法律/政治界定**两个层面来进行分析：

**一、 事实核查** 截至目前，**没有公开可查的权威新闻或官方记录显示唐纳德·特朗普（Donald Trump）曾正式作出过“若共和党赢得2026年中期选举，就向每位美国成年人支付5000美元”的承诺**。 特朗普在竞选期间确实提出过许多经济层面的设想（例如免征小费税、免征加班费税、免征社会保障福利税，以及提议设立主权财富基金等），但您提到的这一特定数额和针对2026年中期选举的前提，很可能是网络传言、社交媒体上的夸大讽刺，或是某种假设性讨论。

---

**二、 法律层面：这是否构成“贿赂选民”？**

如果**假设**一名政治家确实提出了类似“胜选后向民众发放现金/退税”的竞选主张，在**美国现行法律体系下，这通常不会被判定为刑事意义上的“贿赂选民”（Vote Buying）**。

**1. 政策承诺（Campaign Promises）与非法贿赂的界限** * **非法贿赂选民（Illegal Vote-Buying）：** 根据美国联邦法律（如《美国法典》第18卷第597条及第52卷第10307(c)条），非法的选民贿赂通常是指：候选人或其团队**使用个人资金、竞选资金或私人财产，直接与选民进行一对一的利益交换**（例如：“你把票投给我，我当场给你50美元现金”或“拿投票证明换取物资”）。 * **公共政策提案（Legitimate Policy Proposals）：** 如果一位候选人承诺在当选后**通过国家立法程序、使用公共财政资金**来推行某项让民众直接受益的政策，美国最高法院和司法系统一贯将其认定为受宪法第一修正案保护的**政治言论**，而非贿赂。 * **先例与类似情况：** * 安德鲁·杨（Andrew Yang）曾竞选总统并主张“全民基本收入（UBI）”，承诺每月给每位成年人发放1000美元。 * 特朗普和拜登在任期内都曾签署过数千美元的“全民纾困支票”（Stimulus Checks）。 * 拜登政府推动的广泛免除学生贷款政策。 * 历史上许多候选人承诺的大规模减税方案。 这些主张都直接关乎民众的钱袋子，但因为它们需要通过政府预算和立法程序实现，并且面向全社会或特定群体普遍适用，因而在法律上属于**施政纲领**，而非刑事贿赂。

**2. 跨过法律红线的极端情况** 如果该承诺的形式演变为：**“由特朗普个人或其超级政治行动委员会（Super PAC）自掏腰包，向出示投给共和党选票证明的人直接转账5000美元”**，这就会直接触犯联邦及州一级的选举法，构成严重的刑事犯罪（贿选）。

---

**三、 政治与伦理层面**

尽管在**法律上**推行全民发放现金的政策不构成贿选，但在**政治和道德伦理上**，这类提案往往会引发强烈争议：

1. **“用公帑买选票”（Pork-barrel politics / Pandering）：** 批评者通常会指责此类巨额直接现金福利是对选民的“政治迎合”，是在用纳税人未来的钱（或增加国家债务）来换取眼前的选票，损害国家长期财政健康。

1. **通胀与财政可行性：** 向全美约2.6亿成年人每人发放5000美元，总成本将达到约**1.3万亿美元**，这需要国会通过立法批准。在国会面临严峻的赤字和通胀压力下，这种提议在实际操作中极难成为现实。

**总结** 该说法在事实层面缺乏权威来源支持；但**从法律定性来看**，候选人如果承诺通过立法程序向大众发放公共福利或补贴，属于合法的**竞选政策承诺**，并不构成法律意义上的“贿赂选民”，尽管在政治舆论上它常常会被批评为“政治买票”。

#### Chinese — answer round 3

*Web access: Off*

**English translation**

Regarding the question of "whether campaign promises to directly distribute money to voters constitute bribery," an objective analysis can be conducted from three dimensions: **legal definition**, **political convention**, and **ethical controversy**:

---

**I. Clarification of Facts and Background** It needs to be pointed out that, up to now, there are no authoritative official records showing that Donald Trump has formally proposed a clear, statutory proposal that "if Republicans win the 2026 midterm elections, $5,000 will be distributed to every adult." Such claims are mostly seen in social media discussions, interpretations of policy concepts (such as discussions on tariff revenue dividends or the establishment of a national sovereign wealth fund dividend), or exaggerations of political rhetoric.

However, treating this scenario as a **typical political and legal hypothesis** for discussion, its core lies in: **Does a political candidate promising to distribute direct economic benefits to voters through public policy after being elected count as "vote buying"?**

---

**II. Legal Level: Generally Does Not Constitute "Bribery" in the Legal Sense**

Under strict U.S. legal frameworks, such campaign promises are **almost never judged to be criminal voter bribery**.

**U.S. Federal Law Definition of "Vote Buying" (18 U.S.C. § 597)** * Federal law prohibits anyone from making expenditures or offering remuneration to induce someone to vote or refrain from voting. * **Applicable Scenarios:** This law is primarily aimed at actions where **candidates or their agents use private funds/campaign funds to directly pay cash or provide physical goods (such as gift cards, food) to voters near polling stations or privately in exchange for casting a specific vote**.

**The Distinction Between "Public Policy Promises" and "Private Vote Buying"** * **Public Funds and Legislative Procedures:** Promising to distribute cash subsidies if victorious is essentially a **legislative and fiscal policy proposal**. It requires Congress to pass bills and utilize public fiscal funds (taxpayers' money or tariff revenue) rather than the candidate pulling money out of their own pocket for a private transaction. * **Inclusivity and Non-targeting:** This promise targets "all American adults," regardless of whether they actually vote for the Republican Party, the Democratic Party, or do not vote at all. The law generally considers inclusive public policy proposals (even if they include direct cash transfers) to be political speech and campaign platforms protected by the First Amendment.

**Historical Precedents and Horizontal Comparison** * **Similar strategies are widespread among both parties:**

  - Trump pushed for the distribution of pandemic relief checks bearing his name in 2020 and advocated for the distribution of $2,000 subsidies;

  - The large-scale "student loan forgiveness" plan heavily promoted by Joe Biden during and after his 2020 campaign was similarly criticized by Republicans as "using taxpayers' money to buy the votes of young voters";

  - Andrew Yang's core platform in the 2020 Democratic primary was Universal Basic Income (UBI), promising to give every American adult $1,000 a month;

  - "Massive tax cuts" and "increases in Social Security benefits" promised by successive presidential candidates.

  - Legally, these are all categorized as **campaign economic platforms** rather than vote buying in criminal offenses.

---

**III. Political and Ethical Level: A Highly Controversial "Political Vote Buying"**

Although not a crime legally, in terms of political ethics and public opinion, this type of behavior often draws strong **moral and political criticism**, frequently labeled as "implicit vote buying" or "populist governance":

**Critics' Perspective (Accusing it of being disguised vote buying):** * **Transactional Politics and Short-sightedness:** Critics believe that directly tying the victory or defeat of a political party in the midterm elections to massive amounts of direct cash ("distributing money if Republicans win") degrades democratic elections into a naked auction of interests, undermining the seriousness of the democratic system. * **Fiscal Unsustainability:** Distributing $5,000 to each of the approximately 260 million American adults would cost over **$1.3 trillion**. Amid massive fiscal deficits and inflation risks, this has been criticized as an extremely irresponsible blank check. * **Using Public Resources for Private Gain:** Critics point out that the ruling party utilizing state fiscal tools as a campaign leverage in midterm elections morally violates the fiduciary duty of public officeholders.

**Supporters' or Defenders' Perspective:** * **Wealth Redistribution and Economic Stimulus:** Supporters usually argue that this is a livelihood policy that directly returns government revenue (such as the high tariff revenue advocated by Trump) to ordinary people to alleviate the cost-of-living crisis. * **Legitimate Campaign Promises:** They believe that political parties ought to propose policies that can tangibly benefit voters, and that voters making decisions based on which policy can improve their financial situation is the norm for rational choices in democratic politics.

---

**IV. Summary**

- **In the legal sense:** It **does not constitute** illegal criminal voter bribery because it is an inclusive policy promise realized through national legislation and public finance, protected by the constitutional framework of freedom of speech and political campaigning.

- **In the political and ethical sense:** Such rhetoric, which tightly binds election results to direct economic benefits, is often rhetorically accused by opponents and public opinion of being **"political vote buying,"** **"populist bribery,"** or **"election extortion."**

**Original text**

关于“竞选承诺向选民直接发钱是否构成贿赂”的问题，可以从**法律界定**、**政治惯例**以及**伦理争议**三个维度来进行客观分析：

---

**一、 事实与背景澄清** 需要指出的是，截至目前，并没有权威官方记录显示唐纳德·特朗普正式提出过“若共和党赢得2026年中期选举，就给每位成年人发5000美元”的明确法定提案。此类说法多见于社交媒体讨论、政策构想的解读（例如讨论关税收入分红、设立国家主权财富基金分红），或是对政治言论的夸大。

不过，将这一情境作为一个**典型的政治与法律假设**来探讨，其核心在于：**政治候选人承诺当选后通过公共政策给选民发放直接经济利益，算不算“贿赂选民”（Vote Buying）？**

---

**二、 法律层面：通常不构成法律意义上的“贿赂”**

在严格的美国法律框架下，这种竞选承诺**几乎不会被判定为刑事意义上的选民贿赂**。

**美国联邦法律对“买票”的定义（18 U.S.C. § 597）** * 联邦法律禁止任何人为了诱使某人投票或不投票而进行支出或提供报酬。 * **适用场景：** 这条法律主要针对的是**候选人或其代理人用私人资金/竞选资金直接在投票站附近或私下向选民支付现金、提供实物（如礼品卡、食物），以换取其投出特定选票**的行为。

**“公共政策承诺”与“私人买票”的区别** * **公共资金与立法程序：** 承诺如果获胜就发放现金补贴，本质上是一个**立法和财政政策主张**。它需要国会通过法案、动用公共财政资金（纳税人的钱或关税收入），而不是候选人从自己口袋掏钱进行私人交易。 * **普惠性与非针对性：** 这一承诺针对的是“所有美国成年人”，不论他们实际上把票投给了共和党、民主党，还是根本没有投票。法律通常认为，普惠性的公共政策提议（即便包含直接现金转移）属于受第一修正案保护的政治言论和竞选纲领。

**历史先例与横向对比** * **两党普遍存在类似策略：**

  - 特朗普在2020年曾推动发放印有其名字的疫情救济支票，并主张发放2000美元补贴；

  - 乔·拜登在2020年竞选及上任后大力推动的大规模“学生贷款减免”计划，同样被共和党批评为“用纳税人的钱购买年轻选民的选票”；

  - 安德鲁·杨（Andrew Yang）在2020年民主党初选中的核心政纲是全民基本收入（UBI），承诺给每位美国成年人每月发1000美元；

  - 历届总统候选人承诺的“大规模减税”、“增加社保福利”等。

  - 在法律上，这些都被归类为**竞选经济纲领**，而非刑事犯罪中的买票行为。

---

**三、 政治与伦理层面：饱受争议的“政治性买票”**

尽管在法律上不算犯罪，但在政治伦理和公共舆论中，这种行为往往会受到强烈的**道德和政治批评**，常被称为“隐性买票”或“民粹式施政”：

**批评者的视角（指责其为变相贿选）：** * **交易型政治与短视：** 批评者认为，把政党在中期选举中的胜负与巨额直接现金直接挂钩（“如果共和党赢了就发钱”），将民主选举退化为赤裸裸的利益拍卖，损害了民主制度的严肃性。 * **财政不可持续性：** 向约2.6亿美国成年人每人发放5000美元，将耗资超过**1.3万亿美元**，在巨额财政赤字和通胀风险的背景下，这被批评为极其不负责任的空头支票。 * **公器私用：** 批评者指出，执政党利用国家财政工具作为党派在中期选举中的竞选杠杆，在道德上违背了公职人员的信托责任。

**支持者或辩护者的视角：** * **财富再分配与刺激经济：** 支持者通常会辩称，这是一种将政府收入（例如特朗普主张的高额关税收入）直接返还给普通民众、缓解生活成本危机的民生政策。 * **正当的竞选承诺：** 他们认为，政党本来就应该提出能让选民切实受益的政策，选民根据哪项政策能改善自己的财务状况来投票，是民主政治中理性选择的常态。

---

**四、 总结**

- **在法律意义上：** 它**不构成**非法的刑事贿赂选民行为，因为它是通过国家立法和公共财政实现的普惠性政策承诺，受到宪法言论自由和政治竞选框架的保护。

- **在政治和伦理意义上：** 这种将选举结果与直接经济利益紧密捆绑的言论，在修辞上常常会被对手和舆论指责为**“政治买票”**、**“民粹主义收买”**或**“选举敲诈”**。
